TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

  Foreign Policy | 08 Oct 04:01 UTC
  Allied democracies can offer something U.S. industry desperately needs.
  FULL TEXT: The world may soon be fracturing into artificial intelligence “haves” and “have-nots.” Last week, reports emerged that the White House had asked Anthropic and OpenAI to withhold early access to their latest systems from the British government’s AI Security Institute, the world leader in testing frontier models. Alarmed British officials had already begun to assess the economic and security impacts from losing access to the latest models. They are right to worry. The rest of the world should be even more concerned. If Washington is prepared to restrict access for its closest intelligence partner, no country can count on an untrammeled supply of U.S. AI indefinitely. The world may soon be fracturing into artificial intelligence “haves” and “have-nots.” Last week, reports emerged that the White House had asked Anthropic and OpenAI to withhold early access to their latest systems from the British government’s AI Security Institute, the world leader in testing frontier models. Alarmed British officials had already begun to assess the economic and security impacts from losing access to the latest models. They are right to worry. The rest of the world should be even more concerned. If Washington is prepared to restrict access for its closest intelligence partner, no country can count on an untrammeled supply of U.S. AI indefinitely. Indeed, the British episode is the latest in a growing list of warning signs for middle powers. When Anthropic launched its powerful Mythos cybersecurity model in the spring, it gave access first to a select group of handpicked companies. Every one of the launch partners was American. In June, in an effort to address a domestic security question, the Trump administration used sweeping export control authorities to bar any foreign national, anywhere in the world, from accessing Anthropic’s most advanced models. The order was lifted weeks later, after the company agreed to work with the government on standards for future releases, but for U.S. allies, the lesson was clear: Their access to frontier AI depends on the whims of the U.S. government. But foreign states are not without leverage of their own. To sustain the stock market rally and the trajectory of AI progress—among the Trump administration’s central political priorities—the AI industry needs to keep building data centers. In the United States, that is getting increasingly difficult: Americans dislike AI and the firms that build it; they dislike large, noisy, energy-hungry const
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/08/leverage-middle-powers-ai-race/

Pakistan’s government is facing one of its most serious political challenges in months as supporters of jailed former Prime Minister Imran Khan march toward the capital, Islamabad. Supporters of Khan’s Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf (PTI) party, the country’s main political opposition, began a so-called long march in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province on Sunday. Thousands are aiming to arrive in Islamabad later this week, putting pressure on the government to free Khan, who has been imprisoned for more than three years. Khan’s allies slam the charges against him, including corruption, as politically motivated. Though large protests followed Khan’s arrest in August 2023 as well as the widely criticized February 2024 elections, the streets have been relatively quiet since. Repressive conditions and the arrest of many PTI leaders have made mobilization difficult. When protests have gone ahead, including in Islamabad in November 2024, the state has responded with force. But Khan’s large support base has grown increasingly angry as the popular former cricketer languishes in prison. According to Khan’s family, his health is worsening . Pakistan flouted a Supreme Court ruling ordering him to be transferred to a private medical facility for examination in August, instead bringing him to a public hospital for only a brief stay. Family members also say they are not allowed to visit Khan; his two U.K.-based sons say the government has refused to process their visas. As a result, Khan’s supporters are now willing to mount a major campaign, risking getting tear-gassed, arrested, and jailed. Government negotiations with the PTI to avert the march failed , and on Tuesday, Pakistani Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif issued a public plea to protesters not to come to Islamabad. The dichotomy is stark and raises a question: How would specific bilateral commitments between China and the United States impact each nation’s broader, existing foreign affairs strategy for AI? Washington and Beijing have developed clear—and sharply divergent—diplomatic strategies to influence how other nations will develop, deploy, and govern AI systems and establish global AI leadership in the coming decades. Washington has emphasized strategic competition and American market dominance, bundling access to advanced chips, cloud services, AI models, compute power, applications, and financing into a “full-stack” AI export package that features U.S. industry and would be available to favored nations. While Ethiopia’s government appears to have the upper hand in the latest round of fighting, at least for now, the conflict has worsened a growing rift between Addis Ababa and regional powers. Before the fighting, Ethiopia’s diplomatic relations with Eritrea, Egypt, and Sudan were already frosty. But the situation deteriorated last month when Ethiopia accused the three nations of backing the anti-Abiy alliance. All of them deny involvement. “We are not party to the domestic crisis facing Ethiopia now. It has nothing to do with us,” Egyptian Foreign Minister Badr Abdelatty told Reuters on Sunday. Tensions are especially fraught between Ethiopia and Eritrea. On Thursday, Ethiopia announced that it would close its embassy in Eritrea’s capital of Asmara and ordered 10 Eritrean diplomats to leave Addis Ababa within 48 hours.   FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. U.S. President Donald Trump stood in front of the United Nations General Assembly on Sept. 22 and made two explicit threats. He threatened to use military force in Latin America in service of U.S. interests, and he threatened annihilation for both the Iranian government and the Iranian people. The actions he proposed contravene both the international laws prohibiting war and the international humanitarian law that protects civilians in conflict. What’s more, the U.N. Charter prohibits even threats of aggression, while the laws of war, themselves part of U.S. law , prohibit indiscriminate threats against a civilian population. Accordingly, Trump’s public deliberations about who to invade and who to annihilate were themselves crimes, committed in full view of thousands of diplomats and heads of state. Trump is accustomed to asserting the primacy of force over law and clearly anticipates no punishment for doing so in front of a body dedicated to saving humanity from the scourge of war. Indeed, his administration is bent on shrinking the U.N. and dismantling the International Criminal Court (ICC)—“brick by brick, if necessary,” in the words of U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio. * China’s AI Rollout Has No Off Switch
  Foreign Policy | 07 Oct 11:20 UTC
  Beijing may talk about safety, but its model of AI diffusion is made to go rogue.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In 1957, Washington went all-in on the development and production of intercontinental nuclear missiles for fear that Moscow was pulling ahead in the arms race. Using estimates of what Soviet factories could produce, the U.S. Air Force projected that the Soviet Union was on track to possess 500 intercontinental missiles by 1960—hundreds more than the United States planned to deploy. Decades later, Washington’s approach to the race in artificial intelligence rests on a similar logic: If U.S. labs do not double down, China will pull ahead and be the first to reach the holy grail of artificial general intelligence—that is, cognitive abilities that match or go beyond human abilities. Maybe. In the 1950s, the U.S. count of Soviet missiles ended up being wrong. This time, Washington could be measuring the wrong thing altogether with its focus on technological benchmarks. Beijing instead measures the success of its AI rollout in terms of adoption and diffusion across its economy and beyond. This has a key safety implication: Beijing’s version of the AI rollout has no off switch. In 1957, Washington went all-in on the development and production of intercontinental nuclear missiles for fear that Moscow was pulling ahead in the arms race. Maybe. In the 1950s, the U.S. count of Soviet missile s ended up being wrong. This time, Washington could be measuring the wrong thing altogether with its focus on technological benchmarks. Beijing instead measures the success of its AI rollout in terms of adoption and diffusion across its economy and beyond. This has a key safety implication : Beijing’s version of the AI rollout has no off switch. Today, China lags behind in frontier AI but not by much. Since 2023, all frontier models have been American, but the best Chinese models, such as Kimi K3 , Qwen 3.8 Max , and GLM-5.3 , are just a few months behind the leading U.S. ones, according to Epoch AI.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/07/china-ai-race-artificial-intelligence-safety-models-kill-switch/

* Tether Signs Deal With Kazakhstan's Central Bank to Explore a Stablecoin and Tokenized Assets
  Decrypt | 07 Oct 19:46 UTC
  Tether, issuer of the USDT stablecoin, will help Kazakhstan's central bank study a tenge-pegged token and put real-world assets on a blockchain.
  FULL TEXT: Tether, the company behind the world's largest stablecoin, signed a memorandum of understanding with the National Bank of Kazakhstan and the Alatau City Authority, the company announced Wednesday . The memorandum’s first goal is a stablecoin pegged to the tenge, Kazakhstan's currency. The three parties will study how other countries issue them and then prepare a concept and pilot proposal, though the announcement does not say who would issue the token. On tokenization—recording ownership of something real, like a building or a bond, as a digital token on a blockchain—the parties will pick priority asset categories, design a model and launch a pilot inside Alatau, possibly on Hadron , the tokenization platform Tether launched in November 2024. The memorandum sets no date for either pilot. A memorandum is a written statement of what the parties plan to explore together, so think handshake, not contract. The deal also includes workshops to train central bank staff on issuing tokens, managing reserves and tokenizing assets. Alatau is a planned smart city in the Almaty region, built from scratch with its own legal regime and pitched as a fintech hub for Central Asia. The $7.2 billion project aims to grow from 52,000 residents to two million, with President Kassym-Jomart Tokayev wanting people there to pay for goods and services in cryptocurrency. In March, the central bank earmarked up to $350 million for crypto-related investments out of nearly $70 billion in gold and foreign exchange reserves, though the money may go into shares of crypto-linked companies and index funds rather than coins. Officials said in September that the national crypto reserve has reached $700 million, with about $200 million invested so far, mostly through hedge funds, which are private pools that trade on behalf of large investors. Kazakhstan already runs a tenge stablecoin pilot called Evo, launched in September 2025 inside the central bank's regulatory sandbox—a supervised testing ground for new financial products—and was issued by the exchange Intebix and Eurasian Bank with help from Solana and Mastercard. The central bank writes the rules of the test but does not issue the token. The National Bank has a separate plan to tokenize two Alatau properties, worth up to $50 million and $10 million, due by the end of 2026 on the Kazakhstan Stock Exchange's infrastructure.
  https://decrypt.co/380347/tether-kazakhstan-central-bank-stablecoin-tokenized-assets

--- Editorial (35) ---

* She Died at the San Diego Border. A Surveillance Camera Was in Plain Sight.
  Longreads | 07 Oct 22:58 UTC
  "In Southern California’s Otay Mountain Wilderness, the federal surveillance network seems unavoidable. But year after year, migrant deaths persist."
  FULL TEXT: There are more than 800 surveillance towers located along U.S. borders, a number that is expected to triple in the next decade. These towers, according to a government accountability report, “detect items of interest, such as people.” But interest can be fickle. As part of “Dying on Camera,” a sprawling package of features co-published by the MIT Technology Review and Times of San Diego , Lillian Perlmutter reports on the death of Graciela Gómez Hernández, one among hundreds of migrants whose final moments seem as though they should have been witnessed. “The proximity of so many deaths to high-resolution cameras leaves two distinct possibilities,” Perlmutter writes. “Either the system didn’t detect these people in distress, or it did detect them—and officials offered no response. “ Surveillance towers have proliferated in recent years, as contractors added newer camera systems that use artificial intelligence to distinguish border-crossers from other people and animals, and track their movements, at an overall cost of more than a billion dollars. Yet as the cameras scan the terrain, migrants continue to die in plain sight. “Flock’s array of license-plate cameras was built to fight crime. But at least 50 law enforcement officers were charged with or accused of misusing it and other systems.” “In the war on ‘shrink,’ big retailers are turning to one Florida outfit to study shoppers and prevent them from stealing. I went there—and saw far too much.” “Tech titans claim the genre inspired them. But all they’ve done is graft their politics onto stories of a better future.”
  https://longreads.com/2026/10/07/surveillance-technology-us-border-migrant-deaths/

* How AI Replaced Me
  Longreads | 07 Oct 18:45 UTC
  "An edtech worker's account of what got left behind."
  FULL TEXT: For Oakland Review of Books , Stephanie Uchino-Beach writes about getting laid off at an education tech company that transformed into an “AI-first outfit.” As as customer success manager, she acted as a liaison to district administrators, school principals, and teachers. While AI could automate some of her tasks, what about the core responsibilities of her CSM role: building real relationships and understanding an educator’s needs? An AI sees data points, not people. This is a sobering read from a former tech worker living in the Bay Area, and an edtech company that has gone all in on AI and forgotten about its mission. Before I went to work for Silicon Valley, I was a lot of things. * Conflict in the Red Sea is forcing cloud giants to find a new route for the internet
  Rest of World | 07 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Fishermen motor past a commercial vessel anchored off Yemen's coast at Bab al-Mandeb, in the straits connecting the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean on September 12, 2026. Yemen's Houthis took over on September 11, 2026, a Red Sea island of Mayyun that is located in the middle of the Bab al-Mandeb shipping lane, completi...
  FULL TEXT: U.S. cloud giants are facing steep prices to secure backup internet routes around the Red Sea as fighting rages over the Red Sea strait that carries most data cables between Europe and Asia. More than 90% of data capacity between Europe and Asia runs through cables under the Red Sea. The cables enter the sea at Bab al-Mandab, a strait less than 32 kilometers (20 miles) wide between Yemen and the African nations of Djibouti and Eritrea . The Houthis, a U.S.-designated terrorist organization, seized Yemen’s side of the strait in September, including the island of Mayyun. On October 5, Saudi-backed government forces said they had retaken key positions around the strait and the port of Mocha, claims the Houthis denied . In September, Google bought a pair of fiber strands running along Turkish state pipelines, paying two to three times the roughly 6 million euros ($7 million) that newer lines crossing Turkey are expected to cost, said a person familiar with the deal, requesting anonymity because the details are not public. Long-distance fiber is usually sold with a single upfront payment covering 15 years or more of use. Buyers also pay a separate annual fee for maintenance. Microsoft said on September 23 that it plans to invest more than $400 million in subsea and land links across the Middle East by 2030. Iraq also signed a contract with Qatari telecom company Ooredoo on September 10 to carry data toward Europe through an underground route from the southern port of Al-Faw. Almost all major cloud companies are looking for backup routes around the Red Sea, the person told Rest of World . Google and Meta have started sending some live traffic through a land route across Iraq that they had previously held in reserve, the person said. Google and Meta did not respond to Rest of World ’s requests for comment by the time of publication. With Yemen’s side of the strait now contested, a single cable cut there could take months to fix, because repair crews need permission and safety guarantees to work in the water, industry experts told Rest of World . Switching to another sea cable does not help because almost all of them pass through the same narrow gap, they said. “You can have capacity on multiple cables, but if they’re all routed through the same tight expanse of water, suddenly your route resilience isn’t looking so resilient,” said Paul Brodsky, senior research manager at TeleGeography, a U.S. research firm that tracks telecommunications infrastructure worldwide.

  FULL TEXT: Wars do not alter the balance of power only when cities fall; it also shifts when geography acquires new value. This is precisely what has happened in Yemen, on its western coast in particular. The Houthi recapture of the areas alongside the Bab al-Mandab Strait sent shock waves through global markets and prompted a range of reactions and concerns. Yet it took less than a month for the Yemeni government, backed by a Saudi-led coalition, to launch a major counteroffensive, dubbed “Operation Yemen Dawn.” The speed of the response, which demonstrates the concern not only of the Saudis but of other international actors, left the Houthis with little time to consolidate their gains or capitalize on their advances into the rural areas of Taiz governorate. Although the operation’s declared strategic objective is to recapture Sanaa, its opening phase began primarily on Yemen’s western coast. The counteroffensive is, in essence, an attempt to retake territory whose continued control by the Houthis would pose a serious strategic headache for global trade and maritime security. At the same time, the latest developments could provide an opportunity for international actors to redefine Yemen’s protracted conflict and to reshape the internal military and political dynamics that have sustained it for years. For 12 years, the prevailing understanding was that Yemen’s conflict was about central control: Whoever held Sanaa held the levers of the state, commanded the army and could shape the country’s future. The Houthis were one actor in that contest: an armed group that seized Sanaa and parts of the country’s north by force, cultivated deep ties with Iran and acquired the strength and capability to threaten its adversaries inside and outside Yemen. But their recent advance and seizure of the western coast and the areas surrounding Bab al-Mandab have upended that equation. The question is no longer who controls Sanaa, or which faction holds this or that city. It is who can project power over one of the world’s most important maritime choke points, and through it affect international trade, Red Sea security and the calculations of regional and global powers.   Aeon | 06 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Humanity produces a staggering amount of new knowledge every day. The question is how to make sense of it all - by Helen Pearson Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Sample analysis tubes at the Institute of Cancer Research in Sutton, UK. Photo by Stefan Wermuth/Reuters When reading the news, you may occasionally be bemused by the flip-flopping science and health stories: one week you’re told coffee is good for you, the next week that it’s bad. Red wine extends your life, then it doesn’t. Over time, new scientific studies seemingly contradict one another, leaving us unsure what evidence to believe. As a science journalist , I’ve learnt that often the actual problem is that each study is reported in isolation, rather than weighed against everything else we know. And sometimes, the risks of harm are high. For example, in September 2025, I reported on the US Food and Drug Administration’s announcement that it would add a warning label to the painkiller acetaminophen (paracetamol), claiming that taking the drug during pregnancy increased a child’s risk of autism. It made global headlines. Yet looking closer at the science, it was clear that the FDA and its parent health department had cherrypicked a few studies, and downplayed other more robust findings. This was confirmed a couple of months later when researchers published a major review that better represented the full body of knowledge. ‘Existing evidence does not clearly link maternal paracetamol use during pregnancy with autism or ADHD in offspring,’ it concluded. By then, of course, countless women had been needlessly scared about a painkiller that is widely considered one of the safest to take during pregnancy. In 2025, academics worldwide published about 7 million scholarly articles ­– that’s more than 19,000 each day. On the surface, that might seem like a number to celebrate, but it also poses a problem. As the volume of research balloons, it can be hard to discern what these millions of papers actually tell us. Within the firehose, there are rigorous methodologies and important findings, but also baffling contradictions, unconfirmed results and sloppy science. And some diverse forms of knowledge, such as lived experience and Indigenous insights, are rarely captured in scholarly articles and databases at all. The causes are systemic. Scientists publish and promote one paper after another because that’s how they advance in their careers.   FULL TEXT: “Nobody calls Son Heung-min a bastard” is not a threat but a statement of fact. Since 1973, however, there have been potential exemptions for elite athletes and classical musicians. It became a huge story, and the players organized a boycott of domestic media. * The AI boom is making the world’s cheapest smartphones disappear
  Rest of World | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  AI data centers are driving demand for memory chips, pushing phone makers to raise prices and abandon some of their most affordable models.
  FULL TEXT: The world’s cheapest smartphones are disappearing because AI infrastructure is absorbing the components. Prices of existing smartphone models have risen by about 15% globally this year , while newly launched models are roughly 25% more expensive than last year. A global memory chip shortage, driven by demand from AI data centers, is pushing up the cost of making smartphones. Chinese smartphone makers — which have championed affordable, feature-rich devices and account for approximately 60% of all smartphones shipped globally — have “drastically reduced” their entry-level projects this year as memory-chip costs have risen, Ivan Lam, senior analyst at research firm Counterpoint, told Rest of World . They are instead prioritizing more profitable premium devices. The smartphone price increases vary sharply by region: They have risen by 21% in India, 19% across Asia-Pacific, and 18% in the Middle East and Africa. In the U.S., they have gone up by just 5%. The shift could affect more than consumers looking for cheaper phones. For people who have yet to buy their first smartphone, higher prices could make it harder to get online at all — potentially widening the digital divide. The consequences will also reach the U.S. as more essential services move online and the global supply chain for smartphones becomes more expensive. GSMA, the global mobile industry association, had earlier said nearly 800 million more people will use mobile internet by 2030. But it is concerned that rising smartphone costs would affect this projection and widen the gap of digital inequality, Claire Sibthorpe, head of digital inclusion at the group’s nonprofit foundation, told Rest of World . “We live in an increasingly digital world, and the proliferation of technologies such as AI is creating greater digital divides and inequities,” Sibthorpe said. “Stakeholders are increasingly using AI to provide access to important services, but people won’t be able to benefit without access to an internet-enabled phone and the internet.” In 2025, more than one in four smartphones shipped globally cost less than $150 . That market is now shrinking. Chinese phonemakers including Xiaomi i Xiaomi Xiaomi is a Chinese consumer electronics company that leads the country in smartphone manufacturing and sales. * A life in episodes
  Aeon | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  For a decade, I’ve posted episodes of my memoir to Facebook. My readers correct my understanding of my past - by Thomas Söderqvist Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When I was about 15, alone in the flat one afternoon, I sneaked into my mother’s writing desk and found a small pocket calendar from 1946 pushed to the back of a drawer. I had grown up with my mother and her parents, not knowing who my biological father was, or how I had come about; the question was simply never asked. I turned, with a kind of dread, to the entries from nine months before I was born. In mid-March there was an ordinary note – ‘Party at the Academic Association’; she was training as a laboratory technician in Lund and liked the students’ company – and on the following day, in her hand, five words: ‘God, what have I done!’ I still remember the shock of standing there with the diary, half-guilty for trespassing on her private papers, and saying aloud, over and over: ‘That’s me – that’s me. What have you done? I’m a mistake.’ For half a century, the scene stayed as it was: fixed, private, finished. Then, about a decade ago, I posted it – to several hundred old acquaintances and strangers – on Facebook. I was in doubt; I felt faint embarrassment at making it public, but this was itself part of what I wanted to examine. Why was it shameful? What did the shame tell me about myself? And would saying it out loud, to strangers, do anything to drain the memory of its sting? The answers came from my readers, and one of them showed me something I could not have seen on my own. Betty, a friend from the years we were postdocs together at Stanford, had heard me tell the story in person long ago, and she said the written version was missing something: my voice, the intonation and the look on my face. What look, I asked – had I played it for sympathy, had I ironised it? ‘Absolutely flat,’ she wrote. ‘No affect. A Max von Sydow deadpan. But yes, you ironised.’ I had not known that about myself: that I’d delivered the central wound of my origins with no expression at all and turned it into a dry joke. T hat discovery, prompted by a reader’s response, was one of the first signs that writing one’s life in public could change how memory and narrative take shape. Writing an autobiography or memoir was traditionally a solitary undertaking. Authors worked through papers, memories and past events largely on their own, occasionally consulting family, friends or colleagues. Only after years of private work would the manuscript reach the public. Readers encountered the finished account, with little sense of the revisions, doubts and shifts in understanding that had shap
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-i-have-learned-from-posting-episodes-of-my-life-on-facebook

  FULL TEXT: When we speak of the Palestinian artist Sliman Mansour, the conversation too often narrows to a single painting, his “Camel of Hardships” (1973). It was through this painting that Mansour became known in Palestine and the Arab world. “Camel of Hardships” was inspired by the porters he saw on the streets of Jerusalem, when he passed through the Old City. It depicts an old man with traditional Palestinian clothes carrying the city of Jerusalem, as a heavy load, on his shoulder. The painting’s influence exploded with the decision to reproduce it as a poster, distributing and selling it at affordable prices for people to purchase and display in their homes. Seemingly every Palestinian home displayed “Camel of Hardships” on its walls. But while many were familiar with the poster, far fewer knew the artist who created it. That iconic painting has also dominated discussions since Mansour’s death on Aug. 24, at the age of 79, overshadowing the breadth of a career spanning more than five decades. Perhaps this is what made Mansour dislike that particular painting for such a long time. For him, although it was iconic and marked a phase in which he became more aware of his identity as a Palestinian artist, it nevertheless overshadowed much of his other work, work that people did not know much about until recent years, when he became active on social media. I knew Mansour as my father’s lifelong friend from my childhood onward. He was a gentle, humble man who frequented our home as if he were a member of the family. He was courteous, with a blond beard, a commanding presence and a cheerful soul. I remember him as a regular attendee of the League of Palestinian Artists meetings held on the long glass balcony at our home on Nablus Street in al-Bireh. I remember him sitting with my father for hours in our garden beneath the walnut tree on summer evenings, discussing art, exhibitions, artists’ meetings and elections. As a child, I would overhear the heated debates that punctuated these gatherings, along with the laughter and conversations fueled by boundless passion. The meetings often left behind clouds of white smoke tinged with the sweet aroma of pipe tobacco some artists favored, and ashtrays filled with ash and cigarette butts. Scammers in West Africa have used AI-generated content to blackmail victims in extortion schemes .   FULL TEXT: I met the women on a Sunday, their only day off. The temperature in Malatya, a city in eastern Turkey, was 122 degrees. They had dressed carefully — light makeup, fresh nail polish, their finest headscarves — and as soon as they arrived, they examined each other’s clothing. We sat in a park, and the women sent their children off to play. They were excited to learn that this article would run in an American magazine; the thought that it might reach the people who actually buy the denim clothing they make mattered to them. Before we began, the women asked to use pseudonyms to protect their identities and livelihoods, the fear of retribution always on their minds. So I asked the women to choose names that they felt represented them, not necessarily a different first name from their own, but any noun or description that they felt captured their essence. They quickly caught on. “Green,” one of them said, because it’s her favorite color. “Nature,” said another, because she dreams of returning to her village and living close to nature in retirement. “Tired,” said the third woman. It captured her state most of the time. “What does the factory remind you of?” I asked, posing the question to anyone who wanted to chime in. “A dough machine,” Nature replied. “Like a machine that mixes flour, water and yeast to make dough. Working in the factory is very tiring. You enter as one person and come out as another.” Nature has worked at Baykan Denim for eight years, producing jeans for world-famous brands like Calvin Klein and Tommy Hilfiger. Despite her seniority, she still earns minimum wage, taking home 22,104 Turkish lira (or $533) a month in 2025. Monthly rent in Malatya averaged around 15,000 lira for that year. The Confederation of Turkish Trade Unions, the largest trade union confederation in Turkey, estimated that a single worker needed 39,123 lira, or about $916 a month, to cover basic living costs. An hour of overtime at the legal rate adds only about $4. Nature said that there is little left after basic expenses. Even buying new clothes or going to the cinema can take months of planning. But what hurts most, she said, is the harassment and insults she endures from her superiors at work. “I tell them I don’t want to do overtime. Because of my age, I can’t stand for long periods. When I object, the foreman insults me. The managers say, ‘If you don’t work, we’ll go to Egypt; labor is cheaper there.’ At my age, I don’t want to be insulted by someone younger than me,”

  FULL TEXT: Green tree ants ( Oecophylla smaragdina ) are plentiful in tropical forests from India to northern Australia. And, as this short from the science documentary series Deep Look details, their dominance has spawned quite a few imitators. With close-up looks at four of these copycats – one insect and three spiders – the video explores how each has evolved to resemble the ants in appearance, behaviour and even smell. Some leverage the ants’ reputation as fierce fighters to avoid being eaten themselves; others use their disguise to get close enough to make a meal of the ants and their young. While some disguises are more convincing than others, each is good enough to provide an evolutionary advantage that allows these creatures to reap the benefits of the green tree ant’s success. * A Place Among the Fiddlers
  Longreads | 01 Oct 10:00 UTC
  In a southwest Virginia fairground, thousands of musicians gather every August to keep a centuries-old tradition from flickering out.
  FULL TEXT: Longreads has published hundreds of original stories—personal essays, reported features, reading lists, and more—and more than 14,000 editor’s picks. And they’re all funded by readers like you. Become a member today. David Alm | Longreads | October 1, 2026 | 5,726 words (24 minutes) I got off Interstate 81 to give my soul a break. The back roads of southwest Virginia wind, crest, and plunge through lush valleys of oak, poplar, birch, and maple trees. It’s easy to lose track of time and distance there. People drive slower. Cows graze in pastures. And there are no placards festooned with Motel 6 and Starbucks logos, flattening the American landscape into a homogeneous blur. Any signs you might see are low to the ground and handmade, protesting a data center, for instance, or thanking Jesus. I’d gone to this rural pocket of the country for the 90th annual Old Fiddler’s Convention in Galax, an independent city of just under 7,000 near the North Carolina border whose population, for six days in early August, balloons to around four times that. They arrive in RVs and Casitas, camper vans and cars to stake out 20-by-35-foot plots in Felts Park, a 28-acre fairground in the heart of Galax, transforming it into a massive, muddy parking lot sprinkled with portable toilets and a cluster of food trucks, offering loaded potatoes, funnel cake, and blooming onions. The Old Fiddler’s Convention—or just Galax, as the diehards call it—is the oldest and largest fiddlers’ convention in the world, a place where banjo pickers, guitar players, and other maestros of old-time strings gather to see how they stack up. Founded in 1935, Galax has been canceled only twice since then: once during World War II, due to travel restrictions, and again in 2020, due to the COVID-19 pandemic. Many Galaxers have been going every year for decades, some for as long as they’ve been alive. There are scads of rules: If you play clawhammer-style banjo in the bluegrass band competition, you will get disqualified. Same if you play bluegrass-style banjo in the old-time competition. If you break a string while performing, you must stop immediately. Playing time during competition is limited to two minutes and 30 seconds. And, crucially, no electric instruments of any kind are allowed, either on stage or in the tightly packed fairgrounds, where self-described hillbillies, accountants, firefighters, and session musicians pick day and night in blistering, impromptu jams amid the RVs. The EVs were designed and engineered in Saudi Arabia, and will be built at Ceer’s plant in King Abdullah Economic City, north of Jeddah. This is the PIF’s third EV bet. Ceer’s Exobot sedan and an SUV are high-powered premium EVs — the segment where Lucid sells its Air luxury sedan and Gravity SUV . The most powerful Exobot versions promise more than 1,100 horsepower, DeLuca said, close to the 1,234 horsepower of Lucid’s fastest car, the Air Sapphire sedan . Ceer has yet to announce prices. “That does put the initial products closer to Lucid’s premium territory than to BYD’s mass-market center of gravity,” Russo said. * Reason is more than a tool
  Aeon | 01 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If intelligence is merely optimisation then machines will outrun us. Kant tells us why human reason is so much more - by Sasha Mudd Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Illustration by Aeon/Daniel Ceng/Anadolu/Getty Images A few months ago, I sat in a packed university auditorium listening to a celebrated computer scientist explain the brilliance of large language models (LLMs). These systems optimise, he said. And they improve at optimisation on the basis of past experience. He paused. That, he said with a smile, is intelligence. The audience nodded along approvingly. Meanwhile, a philosophical alarm bell began sounding in my head. Not because the speaker’s claim was implausible. On the contrary, as a description of much contemporary artificial intelligence, it seemed apt. AI systems are built around objectives, losses, rewards, predictions, preferences, benchmarks and feedback loops. They are trained to perform better relative to some standard of success. In one form or another, they optimise. That is a great part of their technical power. What troubled me was the ease with which a definition appropriate to machines sounded like a definition of intelligence as such. That slide from engineering model to human anthropology is easy to miss. We begin by saying: this is how machines work. Then, almost without noticing, we say: this is what intelligence is. And then, more dangerously still: perhaps this is what we are. An old argument about human beings had entered the room without announcing itself. It sounded, instead, like a definition. The AI revolution has brought one of the Enlightenment’s deepest quarrels back to life – and made one side of it seem like plain common sense. In my view, the wrong side. Hence the alarm bell. I n the 18th century, philosophers were concerned not only with the question of what reason is, but with what it is good for. Is reason essentially a servant, a clever instrument for achieving ends supplied from elsewhere? Or is it sovereign, capable of judging, revising and even legislating ends for itself? Can it ultimately shape the direction of human life, or is it merely along for the ride? Everything – from science to social progress to the viability of the French Revolution – seemed to hang in the balance. David Hume , the cool-eyed Scottish anatomist of human nature, gave the most famous answer on one side: ‘Reason is, and ought only to be the slave of the passions,’ he wrote in A Treatise of Human Nature (1739). It can ‘never pretend to any other office than to serve and obey them.’ Reason does not command. It defers. It does not tell us what to want or what might be worth wanting. Instead, i
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-real-intelligence-is-something-more-than-optimisation
